=== PROMPT VARIANT ===

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a sequence A = (A_1,\ldots,A_N). You can perform the following two types of operations any number of times in any order:

- Let K be the length of A just before the operation. Choose an integer i such that 1 \leq i \leq K-1, and swap the i-th and (i+1)-th elements of A.
- Let K be the length of A just before the operation. Choose an integer i such that 1 \leq i \leq K and all the values from the 1-st through the i-th elements of A are equal, and delete all the elements from the 1-st through the i-th of A.

Find the minimum total number of operations required to make A an empty sequence.
You are given T test cases; solve each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Each case is given in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answer for each test case in order, separated by newlines.

Constraints


- 1\leq T\leq 10^5
- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq N
- The sum of N over all test cases is at most 2\times 10^5.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
5
1 1 2 1 2
4
4 2 1 3
11
1 2 1 2 1 2 1 2 1 2 1

Sample Output 1

3
4
8

For the 1st test case, A can be made empty by the following three operations:

- Swap the 3rd and 4th elements of A. Now, A is (1,1,1,2,2).
- Delete the 1st through 3rd elements of A. Now, A is (2,2).
- Delete the 1st through 2nd elements of A. Now, A is an empty sequence.

For the 2nd test case, A can be made empty by deleting the 1st element four times. Also, it is impossible to make A empty in three or fewer operations.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's recall: Given colored balls, sort by color using adjacent swaps minimizing swaps, color order arbitrary. Is this NP-hard? I think "Minimum color segregation" maybe NP-hard? Number of colors can be large, NP-hard? Let's verify. We have sequence of colors, want permute colors into blocks minimizing inversions. This is exactly "minimum feedback arc set in tournament"? For each pair of colors a,b, if we order a before b, cost = # b before a. If b before a, cost = # a before b. We need find a linear order of colors minimizing sum over pairs of cost. This is the "minimum feedback arc set" on a complete directed graph with weights w_{a,b} = # (a before b in A)? Actually cost of ordering a before b is number of inversions between a,b = # b before a in A. Let w_{a,b}=# pairs (a before b in A). If order a before b, pay w_{b,a}; if b before a, pay w_{a,b}. Inversions between identical values don't matter; matching can be chosen. The rank model with labeled elements assumes each occurrence distinct; outputting later v before earlier v incurs inversion cost between identical labels, but since values same, maybe we can relabel to avoid cost. In actual adjacent swaps, swapping identical elements has no effect and should not cost. Our labeled model overcounts if we reverse identical elements. But in deletion order, identical elements indistinguishable; we can always choose the order of identical occurrences in P to be their original order without changing value sequence and without increasing inversions? Is that true? Given any value sequence P, if two identical occurrences appear in reverse original order, swapping them in P (same values) leaves value sequence identical, and can only decrease or keep inversion distance to A (by standard stable matching). Thus there exists optimal P where occurrences of each value appear in original order. Therefore we can assume stable order for identical values. In the pick-by-rank model, if we output later v before earlier v, the value sequence could be realized by stable order; but the rank cost may include crossing identical elements which can be avoided by matching. Does the pick model with labels and cost rank among remaining labels overcount? Let's test. Original [v1, x, v2]. If output v2 first, rank among remaining labels =2 (v1 and x before). Then output v1, reward. Sum ranks=2. But value sequence P = [v,v,x]? Actually output v2 then v1 then x gives values [v,v,x]. Stable matching: original v1 -> first v, original v2 -> second v, original x -> last. Inversions: original order v1<x<v2; target v1<v2<x? Wait target values [v,v,x], stable matching v1 to pos1, v2 to pos2, x to pos3. Original v1 before v2 before x; target v1 before v2 before x, no inversions! But the labeled output v2 before v1 would require swapping identical v's, which is unnecessary. The value sequence [v,v,x] can be achieved by outputting v1 then v2 then x with cost0. So any time we output same value consecutively, we should output them in original order to avoid cost. In pick model, to get reward between two v's, if we output the later v before the earlier v, we pay extra but can instead output earlier v first? But earlier v may not be at front; outputting earlier v first might cost less? Let's examine. Original [x, v1, y, v2]. To get v,v consecutive, stable order v1 then v2. We could output v1 first (rank1 because x before), then v2 (remaining [x,y,v2], rank2? Actually after removing v1, remaining [x,y,v2], v2 rank2) total ranks3, reward1. Alternatively output v2 first (rank3), then v1 (remaining [x,v1], rank1) total4, reward1. Worse. So stable order is better. Thus we can restrict to picking occurrences of same value in original order. But wait if v1 not at front, outputting v1 first costs number before it; outputting v2 first costs more because includes v1 and more. So stable is cheaper. Good.

Therefore in optimal P, for each value, occurrences appear in original order. Then the pick-by-rank model with labeled elements and stable order is okay if we enforce that we never pick a later occurrence of a value before an earlier occurrence of same value. But is that always without loss? Yes because identical values indistinguishable; stable matching gives no larger inversion distance. Need prove but fine.

Now, with stable order per value, the output order P is a linear extension of the partial order that for each value, earlier occurrence before later occurrence. Inversions only between different values whose relative order changes. The pick model with labels and cost rank among remaining labels would count inversions between same value if we pick later first; we disallow. If we always pick occurrences of each value in original order, then no same-value inversions. The rank cost among remaining labels includes same-value earlier occurrences before later ones; but if we pick earlier first, those are removed before later, so no cost. Good.

Now problem: We have N labeled elements with partial order chains per value. Need choose an interleaving (merge of chains) minimizing sum of current ranks? Wait if we process output order left to right, and pick an element, inversion cost = number of remaining elements before it in original. This includes elements from other chains and same chain before it. But if we respect chain order, same-chain before it have already been output (because earlier occurrence before later), so not remaining. Thus cost counts only different-value elements before it in original that are not yet output. This is correct.

This is equivalent to: We have a sequence of colored items. We can delete items in any order subject to each color's items deleted in original order. Deleting an item costs the number of not-yet-deleted items to its left (all colors). Reward 1 if same color as previous deleted item. Need minimize cost + N - reward. Since cost is exactly adjacent swaps to transform A to deletion order.

This looks like a scheduling problem with precedence chains and sequence-dependent reward. Maybe can be solved greedily? Let's explore.

Because cost of deleting an item = number of remaining items before it. If we delete items in original order, cost0. If we ever delete an item out of original order, we pay for each skipped item. This is like we can "jump" over a prefix of remaining items at cost equal to number skipped. Reward for consecutive same color encourages deleting same color items together. To delete two same color items consecutively, after deleting the first, the second must be at the front of remaining items (rank0). That means all items between them in original (and not deleted) must have been deleted before the second. If the first and second are consecutive occurrences of color c in original (no other c between), then to output them consecutively, after deleting first, all items between them must already be deleted. They can be deleted before the first? Let's analyze.

Suppose original positions: ... c at p, some items B between p and q, c at q. We want output c at p then c at q consecutively (stable order). At time after outputting p, q is at front iff all items between p and q have been deleted (and no items before p remain? Wait items before p in original may remain; they are before q too, so q would not be at front if any item before p remains. But if there are items before p not deleted, they are before q, so q's rank >0. To output q immediately after p, we need all remaining items before q in original to be deleted. That includes items before p as well. But p was just output; items before p could have been deleted earlier or maybe none. If there are items before p remaining, q not front. Thus to get consecutive same c, we must have deleted all items that are before q in original except p? Actually p is before q and just deleted, so remaining before q are all original positions < q except p and any deleted. Need none remaining. Therefore all items before q must be deleted before outputting q. That means when we output q, it is the first remaining item in original order. So consecutive same pair (p,q) can occur only if q is the first remaining item at that time and p was just deleted. This implies before outputting p, q was second? Let's see.

In sample3, output P: first two 1s are original pos1 and pos3. Before output pos1, remaining starts pos1; after deleting pos1, remaining before pos3 includes pos2=2, so q=pos3 not front. But in the actual pick sequence, after output pos1, we output pos3 immediately? Wait earlier pick sequence: Pick pos1 (rank0), then pick pos3 (rank1), reward. So q was not rank0; rank1. But output P values: first two are 1,1. Inversion cost for picking pos3 was 1 (pos2 before it). So consecutive same did not require q at front; it required q be the first among remaining? No, rank1 means pos2 before q. But output order P has pos3 immediately after pos1, before pos2. That means pos3 moved before pos2, paying inversion. So q not at front; it crossed pos2. The reward is for adjacent in P, not necessarily q at front in remaining before pick; after picking pos1, remaining order [pos2,pos3,...], picking pos3 costs1 and makes P pos1,pos3. So consecutive same in P can be achieved by picking q even if not front, paying inversions with intervening items. The intervening items remain for later. So my previous "q at front" wrong: P is output order, not current sequence after swaps? Actually if we output pos3 before pos2, then in P pos3 before pos2, inversion. This is allowed. In original operations, we would swap pos3 left across pos2, then delete pos1,pos3? Wait after swapping pos3 left, sequence becomes pos1,pos3,pos2,...; delete first two 1s, then pos2 remains. Yes.

So consecutive same in P means after outputting p, we choose q next, paying rank(q) among remaining before deletion. It need not be front; rank cost accounts for crossing intervening items. Thus reward for same value can be obtained by paying inversions to bring q next.

This is exactly the pick model.

Now, can we solve pick model efficiently? It resembles "minimum cost linear extension of disjoint chains with adjacent equal rewards" where cost of picking an element is number of remaining elements before it. There may be a greedy: At each step, choose an element that minimizes current cost minus future reward? Maybe.

Let's compute cost of picking an element at original position p when some set S of earlier positions already deleted. Cost = p-1 - |{j<p: j deleted}|. This is the number of undeleted elements before p. If we process output order, total cost = sum over elements of number of undeleted before it at its deletion time. This is equal to number of pairs (i<j) such that j is deleted before i (inversion). So cost only depends on relative deletion order.

Reward = number of adjacent pairs in deletion order with same value.

This is like we choose a permutation (linear extension of chains) to maximize reward - inversions. This is a known problem maybe solvable by dynamic programming over "frontier" if chains are intervals? But values arbitrary.

Let's formalize local effect. A swap of adjacent elements at positions i,i+1 with values a,b (a≠b) changes run count by Δ = -2, -1, 0, 1, 2? Let's derive using equal adjacencies. Let L = value at i-1 (if exists), R = value at i+2 (if exists). Equal adj count before e = [L==a]+[a==b=0]+[b==R] = [L==a]+[b==R]. After swap order b,a: e' = [L==b]+[b==a=0]+[a==R] = [L==b]+[a==R]. Runs change = e - e' (since runs=N-e). Good if e' - e =2 (runs decrease2): requires L==b and a==R, and L!=a? If L==b and b!=a so L!=a; R==a and a!=b so R!=b. So pattern L=b, a, b, R=a. That is values b,a,b,a. Good.
Runs decrease1 if e' - e =1: exactly one of L==b, a==R true and the corresponding old false. Runs increase similarly.

A swap can reduce runs by2 only if local pattern is alternating pair x,y,x,y. This is independent of initial equal adjacencies elsewhere.

If we perform multiple swaps, the total reduction in runs is not simply sum of local reductions because swaps can affect each other's L/R. But maybe the maximum net saving equals maximum number of disjoint "alternating quadruples" where swapped pair is the middle two, with the additional condition that these quadruples are "separated" so they don't interfere. Maybe the problem can be solved by a simpler DP on runs: We have initial runs; a good swap corresponds to choosing an adjacent pair of runs? Let's think in terms of runs, not elements. Adjacent swap within a run? Swapping equal elements no effect. Swapping adjacent elements from different runs (boundary between runs) can alter run boundaries. A good swap pattern x,y,x,y means there are four runs? Actually values b,a,b,a at positions, so runs are single elements if alternating. More generally, pattern b,a,b,a can occur with runs of lengths? The swapped elements are at a boundary between a-run and b-run. For good swap, need L=b and R=a. That means the element a at position i has left neighbor b (so it is at the start of an a-run? If left neighbor b, and previous element a? Wait if L=b and current a, then there is a run boundary before current; current is first element of an a-run. The next element b is first element of b-run? Since current a, next b, boundary. R=a means after next b, an a, so b-run length exactly1? Let's see segment b, a, b, a. The b before current is some b-run; current a is first of a-run? Actually if L=b and current a, yes current starts an a-run. Next element b starts a b-run. R=a means b-run length exactly1 (since next after b is a). Thus good swap swaps the first element of an a-run with the first element of the following b-run, where the b-run has length1 and the a-run after it exists. More generally, if b-run length >1, R would be b not a, so not good. If a-run length >1? Current a is first of a-run; next element b, so a-run length at least1; if a-run length >1, then before current? Wait current is first of a-run, so previous element is b. The a-run length could be >1, but next element is b, so a-run length exactly1. Thus both runs involved are length1? Segment b,a,b,a: the middle a-run length1, b-run length1. The left b could be part of longer b-run? L=b, current a, so left b is last element of a b-run; if b-run length >1, then element before left b is b, but that doesn't affect local. The right a after b is first of an a-run (could be longer). So good swap requires two consecutive singleton runs a,b (length1 each) surrounded by b on left and a on right. It merges left b-run with the singleton b and right a-run with singleton a, reducing run count by2. This is like removing two singleton runs between runs of same colors.

This suggests a run-level greedy: We have a sequence of runs with values and lengths. A good swap can be performed when there are two consecutive runs of length1 with values x,y, and the run before them has value y, the run after them has value x. Then swapping the two singleton elements (which are adjacent at the boundary between runs) effectively moves the singleton x to the right of singleton y? Let's see original runs: ..., Y, X, Y, X, ... where the middle X and Y are length1. After swapping the middle X and Y (the boundary between the two singleton runs), the sequence becomes ..., Y, Y, X, X, ... i.e., the singleton X merges with right X-run, singleton Y merges with left Y-run. The two singleton runs disappear, reducing run count by2. This is exactly like deleting a pattern Y X Y X by swapping the middle X,Y.

This is reminiscent reducing a run-length encoded string by removing pairs of singleton runs that are "sandwiched" between same colors. The number of good swaps possible maybe equals maximum number of such reductions, and since each good swap reduces run count by2 and costs1, total ops = initial_runs - (#good swaps). But after a good swap, the run sequence changes: two singleton runs are removed, and the neighboring runs merge? Actually if left Y-run and right X-run may have lengths; after swap, the singleton Y joins left Y-run, singleton X joins right X-run. The run sequence loses the two singleton runs, and the adjacent runs (left Y and right X) extend. This may create new patterns for further good swaps at the merged boundaries. This is analogous to removing pairs from a run sequence. Could be solved greedily with a stack? Let's explore.

Represent runs as list of (value, length). A good swap corresponds to a local pattern in run sequence:
..., (y, L_y), (x,1), (y,1), (x, L_x), ...
where the two middle runs are length1. After operation, the two singleton runs are eliminated; the left y-run length increases by1, the right x-run length increases by1. Run count decreases by2. Cost +1. Total operations = initial_run_count - k (because each such swap reduces final run count by2 and adds1 swap, net -1). Is that always achievable and optimal? Need also consider if initial runs already have adjacent equal? By definition runs have distinct adjacent values, lengths >=1.

So run-level reduction works.

Now need determine maximum number k of good swaps (each reduces run count by2) that can be performed, where a good swap corresponds to eliminating two consecutive singleton runs with pattern Y,X,Y,X in the run sequence. After elimination, the neighboring runs merge/extend, potentially creating new patterns. This is reminiscent a stack algorithm for reducing a string by removing "XYXY" patterns? Let's formalize.

We have run-length encoded sequence of runs: each run has value v and length len. A "good swap" is possible at a boundary between two consecutive runs if both have length 1? Let's derive in run terms.

Let runs R_1,...,R_m with values c_1,...,c_m (adjacent distinct) and lengths l_i >=1.

A good swap swaps the last element of run i? Wait earlier pattern at element level: b,a,b,a where the middle a and b are the elements being swapped. In run sequence, the swapped elements are the last element of some run? Let's identify.

Suppose run sequence around pattern: ... c_{p}=Y (length maybe >1), c_{p+1}=X (length1), c_{p+2}=Y (length1), c_{p+3}=X (length maybe >1). The elements are: ... Y Y? Actually left Y-run ends with Y, then X-run is one X, then Y-run is one Y, then X-run starts with X. The two middle runs are X (len1) and Y (len1). The adjacent elements to swap are the X (last/only of run p+1) and Y (only of run p+2). After swapping them, the X moves right after Y, so it joins the right X-run; the Y moves left before X, joins left Y-run. Thus the two singleton runs disappear. This requires l_{p+1}=1 and l_{p+2}=1, and values c_p = c_{p+2}=Y, c_{p+1}=c_{p+3}=X. Since adjacent runs have distinct values, c_p=Y != X, c_{p+3}=X != Y. So pattern values: Y, X, Y, X. The middle two runs are X,Y (length1). After elimination, the left Y-run length increases by1, right X-run length increases by1, and the two runs are removed. The run sequence becomes:
..., (Y, l_p+1), (X, l_{p+3}+1), ...
provided p+3 exists. If p+3 doesn't exist (pattern at end Y,X,Y with no right X), then after swap the Y singleton joins left Y-run, X singleton becomes a run at end? Let's check end pattern Y,X,Y (no R). Original runs Y(len?), X(1), Y(1). Swap middle X,Y -> Y,Y,X? Actually elements: ... Y, X, Y. Swap X and Y -> ... Y, Y, X. Runs: left Y-run extends by1, then X-run length1 remains. Run count before3, after2, reduction1. Not a good swap (increase equal adj1). So good requires right X-run exists. Similarly left Y-run must exist? If pattern X,Y,X,Y at beginning? Need left Y-run exists. If no left Y, swapping X,Y at start? Original X,Y,X,Y... swap first two -> Y,X,X,Y? Runs maybe increase? Let's check pattern X,Y,X,Y with no L. Swap first X,Y -> Y,X,X,Y. Equal adj at X,X; before none? Increase1, not good. So need both left and right runs of appropriate values. Thus good swap corresponds to a length-4 window in run sequence with values Y,X,Y,X and the middle two runs length1. It removes those two middle runs.

This is exactly a reduction rule on run sequence:
Y^a, X, Y, X^b  ->  Y^{a+1}, X^{b+1}
where the two middle runs have length1. The number of runs decreases by2. We can apply this rule any number of times; each application costs1 swap and reduces run count by2, so total ops = initial_m - k. Need maximize k (number of reductions) possible.

Is this reduction system confluent? Need compute maximum number of reductions. Since each reduction removes two runs, k <= floor((m-?)/2). But patterns may overlap. Need find max number of applications.

This is similar to reducing a string by rule "aba b?": Actually values Y,X,Y,X with middle X,Y singleton. It removes the middle two runs. In terms of run values, we delete two adjacent runs (X,Y) when the run before has same value as Y and the run after has same value as X. The lengths of the neighboring runs increase, but values remain. This can create new deletions at the new boundaries.

Because lengths of neighboring runs only increase, never decrease. A run can be deleted only if its length is 1 at the time of deletion. If a run has length >1, it cannot be the middle singleton in a good swap. However, a run's length can increase when adjacent singleton runs are deleted, making it even less deletable. Thus only runs that are initially length1 can be deleted; runs with length>1 act as barriers but can absorb adjacent singleton runs. This is important.

So we have a sequence of runs with lengths. We can delete pairs of adjacent runs (i,i+1) if both have length1 and their values are X,Y, and the run before i has value Y, the run after i+1 has value X. When deleted, the neighboring runs' lengths increase by1. This is like removing two singleton runs from between two runs of "opposite" colors.

Can we compute max deletions greedily? Since each deletion reduces run count by2 and requires a pattern Y,X,Y,X. This resembles removing "valleys" in a string where adjacent equal after deletion? Maybe can use stack.

Let's test with run sequence values and lengths.

Can we characterize max deletions as maximum number of disjoint pairs of active runs such that in the original run value sequence, each pair is "matched" with surrounding inactive/active? Let's think.

Each deletion consumes two active runs (the middle pair) and makes its two neighboring runs inactive (if active). Thus a run can be:
- deleted (active at time, consumed)
- blocked (inactive) either initially or after being neighbor to a deletion
- never involved.

If a run is active and not deleted, it remains active at end? It could be neighbor to a deletion later, then blocked. If it is never neighbor, remains active. Active runs that remain contribute to final runs.

Each deletion reduces number of active runs by 2 (deleted) and may reduce active count by up to 2 more (neighbors become inactive). But total run count reduces by2 regardless of neighbor active status. For maximizing k, we want use active runs as deleted pairs; blocking neighbors may reduce future opportunities but is necessary.

This looks like we can model as: We have a sequence of runs with values and active flag. A deletion of adjacent active runs i,i+1 is allowed if the nearest remaining runs to left/right have values equal to run i+1 and run i respectively. After deletion, those nearest runs become inactive (if active). This is exactly like removing a pair from a doubly linked list; the neighbors' active flags set to false.

So algorithm: after pushing each run, while top4 form pattern Y,X,Y,X and the middle two (stack[-3], stack[-2]) are active, delete them (pop those two), mark stack[-4] and stack[-1] inactive, and then continue (because new adjacencies may form with previous elements). For length6, after first deletion stack [1i,2i], read old5,old6 -> before old6 stack [1i,2i,1a]; push old6 -> [1i,2i,1a,2a]. Top4 [1i,2i,1a,2a] pattern 1,2,1,2, middle stack[-3]=2i inactive, so no deletion. Good.

This stack algorithm seems promising. It processes runs left to right, deletes whenever the last four runs form the pattern and the middle two are active. But is greedy "delete whenever possible" optimal? Need test with cases where choosing not to delete now could allow more deletions later. Since stack algorithm deletes as soon as pattern appears at the right end. In such string rewriting systems, greedy may be optimal if the rule is confluent and terminating? Need verify.

The rewriting rule on runs with active flags:
- A run is active if length == 1.
- When a deletion occurs between runs B and C in pattern A,B,C,D with values Y,X,Y,X and B,C active, we remove B,C; A and D become inactive (if active). This is exactly what stack algorithm does when top4 match and B,C active.

But is the greedy "when top4 match, delete immediately" always optimal? This is a local rewrite system. Need prove or find counterexample.

Let's try to construct a case where deleting early blocks two later deletions, while delaying allows more. Since the rule is triggered by a pattern at the right end of processed prefix; if we don't delete, the pattern remains in the stack and may interact with future runs. Could delaying allow a different pairing with future runs? Example pattern Y,X,Y,X; the middle X,Y could instead be paired with future runs? The middle X might serve as left neighbor for a future deletion, or Y as right neighbor for a previous? But if we delete them now, they are gone; if we keep them, they might enable more deletions. Greedy might be suboptimal if a run should be preserved as a neighbor rather than deleted. However, deleting a pair reduces run count by2 and blocks two neighbors. Keeping them might allow two deletions involving them as neighbors? But a run can be neighbor to at most? It can be neighbor to multiple deletions on same side? Let's see.

In a deletion, the two outer runs become inactive. An inactive run can still be neighbor to future deletions (as sample old4 was right neighbor for first deletion and left neighbor for second). So preserving a run doesn't prevent it from being neighbor. Deleting a run removes it. A deleted run could have served as neighbor for future deletions, potentially enabling more than one deletion? But a run can serve as neighbor for multiple deletions while active? If active, first time it serves as neighbor it becomes inactive; it can still serve as neighbor later. So even if not deleted, it can support multiple future deletions. Deleting it removes support. Thus deleting a run might reduce future opportunities. But each deletion requires two active runs to delete; if we don't delete them, they remain active and could be deleted later or support others. Maybe greedy could be bad.

Let's try to prove stack greedy by induction on number of runs processed.

We process runs left to right. Maintain a reduced sequence S (stack) that is equivalent to the processed prefix under some optimal reductions, with the property that no deletion pattern exists entirely within S except possibly involving the last? Actually after each deletion, we continue checking top4, so S is "irreducible" in the sense that no top4 pattern with active middle exists? Wait after processing a prefix, there might be patterns not at the top? The stack algorithm only checks top4 after each push/delete. Could there be a deletable pattern in the middle of the stack that was not deleted? It would have been at the top at the time its right boundary was pushed, so it would have been deleted then. After deletions, new adjacencies can create patterns involving the boundary between the reduced prefix and newly pushed runs; checking top4 after each deletion handles patterns that end at the top. Patterns entirely within the stack but not at top cannot exist because any pattern's right boundary was once the top and would have been processed. Thus S is irreducible (no valid pattern anywhere) after each step? Need check: A deletion can create a new pattern not at the top? It creates adjacency between the outer runs, which are at positions top-3 and top after deletion? Actually after deleting middle two, the outer runs become adjacent at the top of the stack (if the deletion was at the top). The new adjacency is at positions len-2 and len-1? Let's see stack before deletion [..., A, B, C, D] with A,B,C,D top4. Delete B,C -> [..., A, D]. The new adjacency is between A and D at the end. Any new pattern involving this adjacency must include D as the last run (since A,D are at end), so it will be at the top and checked in next iteration. Thus invariant holds.

If S is irreducible, then the number of deletions performed so far is maximum for the processed prefix? Need show any valid sequence for the prefix can be transformed to one that ends with S and same number of deletions. This is the induction. When a new run r is appended to an irreducible S, the only possible new deletions are those involving r and some suffix of S. Since S was irreducible, any deletion in the new sequence must use r or be entirely in S (impossible). Thus we can reduce by repeatedly applying the unique? Actually there may be multiple patterns involving r: top4, maybe after deletion another. The stack algorithm deletes whenever top4 pattern matches. Could there be a choice to delete a different pattern involving r (not top4) that leads to more deletions? Since r is the last run, any deletion involving r must have r as the right neighbor or as a middle run? Wait a deletion of a pair not including r but using r as right neighbor would have pattern ending at r: ..., Y, X, Y, X where r is the last X. This is exactly top4 pattern. A deletion including r as a middle run? The deleted pair could be the last two runs (positions len-1,len) with r as second middle? Pattern ..., Y, X, Y, X where r is the last X? Actually if r is in the deleted pair, it would be one of the middle runs, not the right neighbor. For r to be middle, the pattern would need a right neighbor after r, which doesn't exist yet. So r can only be the right neighbor (outer) in a deletion at this moment. Thus the only possible deletion involving the new run is the top4 pattern where r is the fourth run. Therefore if it matches and middle active, any optimal sequence for the new prefix that performs any deletion must perform this deletion (or maybe no deletion). But we are considering processing prefix; future runs not yet read. In an online greedy, we decide before seeing future. To prove optimal for the entire sequence, we need show performing this deletion now is safe. This is the critical point.

In string rewriting, if a rule is applicable at the right end, applying it is safe if the rewrite system is "optimal" and the rule is not needed to be delayed. We can use an exchange argument: Suppose an optimal sequence for the whole input does not apply this deletion at the time the prefix ends (i.e., before reading further runs). It may apply it later, after reading more runs, or never. If it applies it later, the right neighbor r is still there (unless deleted? r could be deleted later as middle when more runs arrive; but if r is deleted, then the pattern's right neighbor is gone). If r is deleted later, then the deletion we identified (using r as right neighbor) cannot happen later. Could that be better? Need compare.

Can we prove by "lookahead": If a pattern is available, the leftmost possible deletion should be performed because it blocks the left outer run (already in past) and the right outer run, but leaves runs to the right active. Delaying to delete a pattern shifted right would block a right run that might be useful later. So greedy is optimal.

This is analogous to interval scheduling: each deletion corresponds to choosing a pair of adjacent runs; choosing a leftmost pair leaves maximum room to the right. But interactions with blocking complicate.

Let's attempt to model deletions as matching pairs in the original run sequence. Each deletion consumes two runs (middle) and "marks" two outer runs as blocked. The outer runs are immediately adjacent to the pair in the current reduced sequence, which may not be original immediate neighbors if intervening runs deleted. In terms of original indices, a deletion pair can be seen as matching two runs that become adjacent after some inner deletions? Actually the pair deleted are adjacent in current sequence; they may have had other runs between them originally? No, they are adjacent runs in current sequence, so originally there may have been deleted runs between? Wait current sequence is original with some runs removed. If two runs are adjacent in current sequence, all runs between them in original have been deleted. Thus a deletion can involve runs that were not originally adjacent, after the runs between them are removed. This is like a non-crossing matching: each deletion pairs two runs that enclose a set of previously deleted pairs? Let's think.

When we delete a pair of adjacent runs in current sequence, in the original run order, these two runs have between them only runs that have already been deleted (since they are adjacent now). Thus deletions form a non-crossing matching of runs, where each matched pair consists of two runs that are adjacent after removing all inner matched pairs. This is exactly like reducing a string by removing matched pairs; the stack algorithm is natural. The condition on values: for a matched pair (i,j) that are adjacent after inner deletions, with left neighbor L and right neighbor R (nearest surviving outside), values must satisfy value(L)=value(j), value(R)=value(i). Also both i,j must be active (length1) at time of deletion; if a run is outside a deletion, it becomes inactive and cannot be matched later. This is analogous to non-crossing matching with colors.

Could maximum number of deletions be computed by a stack that matches runs when possible, similar to parentheses. The stack algorithm essentially does this.

Now, implement stack algorithm.

Data structures:
- Read N and array A.
- Run-length encode A into list of (value, length). Initial run_count = len(runs).
- We need simulate stack of runs. Each stack element can be a tuple (value, active) where active = (length == 1). But when a run serves as neighbor, we need set active=False. If it's already False, no change. We also need know values for pattern.
- Stack as list of values and active flags maybe two lists or list of [val, active].
- k = 0.
- For each run (val, length) in RLE:
    active = (length == 1)
    push (val, active)
    while len(stack) >= 4:
        # Check top four: stack[-4], stack[-3], stack[-2], stack[-1]
        # Pattern values: v0, v1, v2, v3 should satisfy v0 == v2 and v1 == v3 and v0 != v1 (automatically adjacent runs distinct? In stack, adjacent runs should have distinct values? After deletions, outer runs values are distinct because original adjacent runs distinct and deleted pairs have outer values Y and X with Y!=X. So yes distinct. But check anyway.)
        # Middle two are stack[-3] and stack[-2]; need both active.
        if stack[-4].val == stack[-2].val and stack[-3].val == stack[-1].val and stack[-3].active and stack[-2].active:
            # delete middle two: pop stack[-2], stack[-3]? Need remove indices -3 and -2.
            # Mark outer runs inactive: stack[-4].active = False; stack[-1].active = False.
            # Then remove the two middle elements.
            # Since stack[-1] is the newly pushed right neighbor; after marking inactive, it remains.
            # We can do:
            stack[-4].active = False
            stack[-1].active = False
            # Remove elements at positions -3 and -2. After marking, pop from top? The top is stack[-1], then stack[-2], stack[-3].
            # We need remove stack[-3] and stack[-2], keep stack[-4] and stack[-1].
            # Easiest: stack = stack[:-3] + stack[-1:] ? Wait stack[:-3] keeps up to -4, then stack[-1:] keeps last. But if stack length >4, stack[:-3] keeps all except last3? Let's check: stack[-4] is fourth from end. We want keep elements before stack[-4], stack[-4], and stack[-1]. Remove stack[-3], stack[-2].
            # stack[:len-3] + [stack[-1]]? Since stack[-4] is at index len-4. stack[:len-3] includes indices 0..len-4 (including stack[-4]). Then append stack[-1]. Yes.
            stack = stack[:-3] + [stack[-1]]
            k += 1
            # After deletion, the new last element is the old stack[-1] (right outer), now inactive. The new second last is old stack[-4] (left outer), inactive. Continue loop to check new top4.
        else:
            break
- Answer = initial_run_count - k.

In-place deletion of middle two while keeping top:
We have stack length L. Elements indices:
L-4: left outer A
L-3: middle B
L-2: middle C
L-1: right outer D
We want remove B,C, keep A,D. D is at top. We can do:
# Mark A and D inactive
stack[-4][1] = False
stack[-1][1] = False
# Remove C (index L-2) and B (index L-3). Since D at top remains.
# We can pop D first? Let's do:
d = stack.pop()       # removes D (top)
stack.pop()           # removes C (now top after D removed? Original stack after pop: ... A,B,C. Top is C, so pop removes C)
stack.pop()           # removes B (now top is B)
stack.append(d)       # put D back
# Now stack has ... A, D. A at new top-1, D at top.
This is O(1). Need ensure if L=4, after popping D, stack [A,B,C]; pop C, pop B, append D -> [A,D]. Good.
But note we marked D inactive before popping; d is reference to list, okay.
Then continue while.

Actually D is last, so any pattern must have D as the fourth run (right neighbor) because D is at the end. Thus checking top4 is sufficient. Good.

Now, initial run_count = len(runs). But after deletions, final run_count = initial_run_count - 2*k. Total operations = swaps k + final_run_count = k + initial_run_count - 2k = initial_run_count - k. So answer = initial_run_count - k. This matches.

Let's implement in Python efficiently.

Input T up to 1e5, sum N 2e5. Use sys.stdin.buffer.read().split() for fast parsing.

Pseudo:

import sys
def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it)
        A = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
        # RLE
        runs = []
        for x in A:
            if runs and runs[-1][0] == x:
                runs[-1][1] += 1
            else:
                runs.append([x, 1])
        m = len(runs)
        stack = []  # each [value, active]
        k = 0
        for val, length in runs:
            active = (length == 1)
            stack.append([val, active])
            while len(stack) >= 4:
                # top four
                v0 = stack[-4][0]; a0 = stack[-4][1]
                v1 = stack[-3][0]; a1 = stack[-3][1]
                v2 = stack[-2][0]; a2 = stack[-2][1]
                v3 = stack[-1][0]; a3 = stack[-1][1]
                if v0 == v2 and v1 == v3 and a1 and a2:
                    # delete middle two
                    stack[-4][1] = False
                    stack[-1][1] = False
                    d = stack.pop()  # v3
                    stack.pop()      # v2
                    stack.pop()      # v1
                    stack.append(d)
                    k += 1
                else:
                    break
        ans = m - k
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

Original runs adjacent distinct. Deletion of pattern Y,X,Y,X removes middle X,Y, leaving Y (left outer) adjacent to X (right outer). Since Y != X, adjacent distinct. So yes. If initial runs length>1, still distinct. Thus v0 != v1 and v1 != v2 etc. So pattern Y,X,Y,X with v0=v2=Y, v1=v3=X. Good.

What if stack[-4] and stack[-1] are the same run? No, stack length>=4 distinct elements.

No. If stack length=4, after marking, d = stack.pop() removes D. Then stack.pop() removes C, stack.pop() removes B, append D. Good. The left outer A remains in stack at index0. Its active flag False. Good.

Potential issue: The active flag of a run that is outer in a deletion should be set False only if it was active; if already False, no change. Setting False is idempotent.

Potential issue: A run can be outer in multiple deletions; active remains False. Good.

Potential issue: What if a run is deleted as middle after being marked inactive? We check a1 and a2 active, so no.

In general, deleting elements before swapping removes potential partners for grouping, likely not beneficial. But let's prove to ourselves: Given any sequence of operations, consider the first deletion operation. It deletes a prefix block of value x. Before that, some swaps may have occurred. The elements deleted are the first L elements at that time. If instead we postpone this deletion until after all swaps that occur before it? Hmm.

The P model with all swaps before deletions is standard for sorting with deletions: Since adjacent swaps and deletions commute in a certain sense? If a deletion occurs before a swap, the swap involves two elements that remain. We can move that swap before the deletion without changing the final deletion order? Suppose sequence state S, delete prefix block B, then swap adjacent elements among remaining. If we instead swap those elements in S (they are in same relative order, possibly with B before them), then delete B, the remaining order after swap is the same. The swap cost same. Thus we can commute deletions later than swaps: any swap after a deletion can be performed before that deletion, as long as the swapped elements are not in the deleted block (they aren't, since remaining). Swapping them before deletion doesn't affect the prefix block? If the swapped elements are after the prefix, swapping adjacent among them doesn't change the prefix. So yes, we can move all swaps before all deletions, preserving costs. Therefore there is an optimal strategy with all swaps first, then deletions. Great! This justifies run-level reduction on original A.

Let's articulate: Take any sequence. Find the last deletion? Actually to move swaps before deletions, consider a swap immediately after a deletion. The deleted prefix is before all remaining elements; swapping two remaining elements doesn't affect the prefix, so perform the swap before the deletion instead. The state after deletion+swap is same as after swap+deletion. Repeat to move all swaps before deletions. Thus optimal = sort A to some sequence by adjacent swaps, then delete runs. Good.

Now, after sorting A to P, deletion ops = runs(P). Swaps = inversion distance. We then considered adjacent swaps in P relative to A. The run-level good swaps are exactly adjacent swaps that reduce runs(P) by2 at cost1; any adjacent swap can reduce runs by at most2, so only good swaps improve total. But what about a sequence of swaps where a swap reduces runs by1 and another reduces runs by2, net improvement1; this would correspond to k=1 good swap plus one neutral swap, total operations = initial_runs -1. Our stack counts only good swaps, so answer initial_runs -1. If such sequence exists, stack would give same answer if there is at least one good swap somewhere. But if the only way to get one good swap requires a neutral swap first, stack might count zero? Need revisit with all-swaps-first model.

We start with A (run sequence). We can perform adjacent swaps to transform to P. Each adjacent swap changes run count by Δ (could -2,-1,0,1,2). Cost1. Final total = initial_runs + sum Δ + swaps? Actually final_runs = initial_runs + sum Δ_i (where Δ_i negative for reduction). Total ops = final_runs + #swaps = initial_runs + sum Δ_i + #swaps = initial_runs - sum(reduction_i -1). So the problem reduces to: Given run sequence with lengths, we can perform operations on adjacent singleton runs:

This looks like we can delete singleton runs one by one (neutral) or in pairs (good). Neutral deletion of a singleton run occurs when it is adjacent to a run of the same value on one side? Let's characterize neutral operation in run terms:
Pattern L, X, Y, R with L=Y (and R!=X) deletes the Y singleton (second middle) and merges it with left L. After deletion, sequence L(extended), X, R. The X singleton remains. This is equivalent to removing a singleton run Y that is immediately after an X singleton and preceded by a Y run, with R not X. Similarly if R=X, it would be good (deletes both X and Y). So neutral can remove one singleton run when it is part of a "near-pattern" Y,X,Y with right neighbor not X. It removes the second Y (the one after X), merging with left Y. It costs1 and reduces run count by1.

Alternatively pattern L,X,Y,X with L!=Y removes the X singleton (first middle) merging with right X. This is symmetric.

Let's define reduction rules on stack top4 with active middle:

Let a=stack[-4], b=stack[-3], c=stack[-2], d=stack[-1].

If ab and ac:
    if va == vc and vb == vd:
        # good: delete b,c; mark a,d inactive; k++
        # stack becomes ... a(inactive), d(inactive)
    elif va == vc:
        # neutral left: delete c; mark a inactive; keep b,d
        # stack becomes ... a(inactive), b, d
    elif vb == vd:
        # neutral right: delete b; mark d inactive; keep a,c
        # stack becomes ... a, c, d(inactive)
    else:
        break
Else break.

Let's think of the extended stack as a deterministic reduction. We can maybe prove by induction that it computes the maximum number of good reductions because the reduction system is confluent and the stack normal form has a fixed number of good reductions. The rules are:
- Good: x y x y -> x y (with middle y,x deleted, outer x,y inactive, k++)
- Neutral left: x y x z (z!=y) -> x y z (third x deleted, first x inactive)
- Neutral right: z x y x (z!=y) -> z y x (first x deleted, last x inactive) [or in top4 L,X,Y,X -> L,Y,X]
These are exactly the rules for reducing a string by removing "peaks" where a singleton is between same values. This is similar to reducing a word by deleting a character if it equals the character two positions away, with the intervening character singleton. The number of good reductions might be invariant.

Given complexity, maybe the official solution uses a different, simpler DP that accounts for all swaps. Let's revisit the pick model and see if there is a known polynomial solution.

We need minimize inv(P)+runs(P). This is equivalent to finding a permutation P minimizing inv - adj_eq. Could be solved by dynamic programming over values? But NP-hard concern. However, the operation 2 prefix deletion maybe imposes that P is not arbitrary? Wait we argued all swaps before deletions. But maybe adjacent swaps before deletions can be interleaved with deletions, and the final deletion order P is not arbitrary because deletion operations remove prefixes, but after all swaps before deletions, P is arbitrary. So NP-hard if grouping colors. But maybe the problem is NP-hard? AtCoder wouldn't. Did we mis-model operation 2? It deletes a prefix of equal values, but i can be any up to K, all values from 1st through i-th equal. If we sort A into blocks and delete, yes arbitrary block order. The NP-hard grouping problem would be a special case where we require each color one block; but optimal might split colors, making the problem easier? Could the optimal P always be obtained by a simple stack reduction, avoiding NP-hard? Our extended stack is polynomial. So the problem is not NP-hard because we don't need to group all colors; we only perform local beneficial swaps.

Now, let's consider if the stack should perform neutral reductions even when they don't immediately enable a good reduction. In the above, first neutral enabled good. Let's try to prove confluence of the extended reduction system. The rules are:
1. x y x y -> x y (good, count+1, outer inactive)
2. x y x z (z != y) -> x y z (neutral left, first x inactive)
3. z x y x (z != y) -> z y x (neutral right, last x inactive)
These are deterministic if we always reduce the rightmost possible pattern? The stack algorithm reduces as soon as a pattern appears at the top. This is a left-to-right greedy. If the system is confluent, the number of good reductions is invariant. Is it confluent? Let's test overlapping rules.

Consider string x y x y x. Top4 x y x y -> good deletes middle y,x, leaving x y x. Alternatively top4 y x y x (positions2-5) -> good deletes middle x,y, leaving x y x. Both one good. Good.

Thus neutral doesn't overlap with a good starting at position2 unless z=y (which would be good instead of neutral). Good.

Consider x y x y z. Greedy good deletes middle y,x, leaving x y z. Alternative neutral? Top4 x y x y is good, no neutral. Could there be a neutral left on positions2-5 y x y z if z!=x? Greedy good leaves x y z. Which allows more good reductions? Example x=1,y=2,z=3: original 1,2,1,2,3. Greedy good -> 1,2,3 (k1). I think the extended stack is correct.

Let's implement extended stack.

Pseudo:

stack = []  # [value, active]
good = 0
for val, length in runs:
    active = (length == 1)
    stack.append([val, active])
    while len(stack) >= 4:
        v0,a0 = stack[-4]
        v1,a1 = stack[-3]
        v2,a2 = stack[-2]
        v3,a3 = stack[-1]
        if not (a1 and a2):
            break
        if v0 == v2 and v1 == v3:
            # good
            stack[-4][1] = False
            stack[-1][1] = False
            d = stack.pop()
            stack.pop()
            stack.pop()
            stack.append(d)
            good += 1
        elif v0 == v2:
            # neutral left: delete stack[-2] (v2), mark stack[-4] inactive
            stack[-4][1] = False
            # remove stack[-2]
            # stack: ... A, B, C, D. Remove C, keep A,B,D.
            d = stack.pop()   # D
            stack.pop()       # C
            stack.append(d)   # D
            # Now stack ... A, B, D
        elif v1 == v3:
            # neutral right: delete stack[-3] (v1), mark stack[-1] inactive
            stack[-1][1] = False
            # remove B, keep A,C,D
            d = stack.pop()   # D
            c = stack.pop()   # C
            stack.pop()       # B
            stack.append(c)   # C
            stack.append(d)   # D
            # Now stack ... A, C, D
        else:
            break

Need be careful with in-place removal for neutral left:
Stack before: [..., A, B, C, D] where A=stack[-4], B=stack[-3], C=stack[-2], D=stack[-1]. Remove C, keep A,B,D.
If we do d=pop() (D), then stack [..., A,B,C]; pop() removes C; append D -> [..., A,B,D]. Good. B remains active. A marked inactive.
Neutral right: remove B, keep A,C,D.
d=pop() (D), c=pop() (C), pop() removes B, append C, append D -> [..., A,C,D]. Good. D marked inactive.

But note after neutral left, the kept B is at stack[-2]? Actually stack [..., A, B, D]; B is second from top. It remains active. Good.

After neutral right, C is second from top? [..., A, C, D]; C active. Good.

Now, what if after neutral left, the new top3 A,B,D form a pattern with a future run; loop continues.

Let's test neutral right with example 1,2,3,2,3:
Push 1,2,3,2 -> top4 v0=1,v1=2,v2=3,v3=2. a1,a2 true. v0==v2?1==3 no. v1==v3?2==2 yes -> neutral right: mark D (2) inactive, remove B (2), keep A=1,C=3,D=2. Stack [1,3,2i]. Push 3 -> [1,3,2i,3]. top4 v0=1,v1=3,v2=2,v3=3: v0==v2?1==2 no; v1==v3?3==3 yes, middle a1=3 active, a2=2 inactive -> a2 false, break. k0. Is there a good swap possible in original 1,2,3,2,3? Check patterns: 1,2,3,2 no; 2,3,2,3 good? Values 2,3,2,3 at positions2-5! Wait original runs 1,2,3,2,3. Pattern 2,3,2,3 is good! Did our stack miss it? Let's see original 1,2,3,2,3 all active. Top4 after pushing all 5 runs: stack [1,2,3,2,3]. Top4 indices2-5: 2,3,2,3. Our loop checks top4 of the whole stack: stack[-4]=2, stack[-3]=3, stack[-2]=2, stack[-1]=3. v0=2, v1=3, v2=2, v3=3. v0==v2 (2=2), v1==v3 (3=3), middle active -> good! But wait our earlier processing with neutral right at first four runs 1,2,3,2 did a neutral deletion before seeing the fifth run, altering the stack and missing the good pattern! Let's simulate carefully.

Original runs: 1,2,3,2,3.
Process:
Push 1.
Push 2.
Push 3.
Push 2. Stack [1,2,3,2]. Top4 values 1,2,3,2. v0=1, v1=2, v2=3, v3=2. v0==v2?1==3 no. v1==v3?2==2 yes -> neutral right. It deletes the first middle 2 (run2), marks run4 inactive. Stack becomes [1,3,2i].
Now push 5th run 3 -> stack [1,3,2i,3]. Top4 values 1,3,2,3. No good because middle 2 inactive. But original had good pattern 2,3,2,3 at runs2-5! The greedy neutral deletion destroyed it. This is a counterexample to extended stack greedy! Let's verify actual operations.

A = [1,2,3,2,3]. Initial runs5. Is there a good swap directly? Pattern 2,3,2,3 at positions2-5: swap middle 3 and2 (positions3 and4) -> 1,2,2,3,3. Runs3, swap1, total4. So answer should be 4 (initial_runs5 -1). Extended stack greedy did neutral first and got k0, answer5. So greedy neutral is not safe! Need a different algorithm.

We need maximize good reductions, and neutral reductions should not be done if they destroy a good pattern. In this example, the good pattern was already present in original but not at the top4 when the first four runs were processed; it became top4 only after pushing the fifth run. The stack algorithm performed a neutral reduction on the first four runs before seeing the fifth, which was premature. So we cannot perform neutral reductions greedily without lookahead. Good reductions are safe? If a good pattern is present at top4, performing it is probably safe. Neutral reductions may be unsafe.

Thus we need a better approach.

We need compute maximum number of good reductions, allowing neutral reductions only when they help, but not hurting. This is more like dynamic programming on runs.

Let's analyze the run reduction system more abstractly.

We have a sequence of runs with values and lengths. We can delete runs via operations:
- Good deletion: delete two adjacent singleton runs X,Y when the nearest remaining runs outside are Y,X. Counts +1.
- Neutral deletion: delete one singleton run Y when it is adjacent to a singleton X and the nearest remaining run on one side is Y (and the other side not matching to make good). Counts 0.
We want maximize good deletions. Neutral deletions can be used to remove "blocking" runs, but should not remove runs that are part of a good pattern.

In the example 1,2,3,2,3, the good pattern is 2,3,2,3. The first run 1 is just a left extra. The stack should not reduce 1,2,3,2 because the 2 at position2 is needed as left outer for the good pattern. Neutral right deleted it. So we need lookahead.

This is similar to finding maximum number of non-overlapping good patterns in the run sequence, where runs can be removed by neutral deletions to make patterns adjacent. Maybe there is a simpler DP.

Let's think in terms of original run values and lengths. A good deletion ultimately removes two singleton runs that are adjacent in the current sequence. The outer runs for that deletion are the nearest surviving runs outside. In the example, good deletion of runs3=3 and4=2 had left outer run2=2 and right outer run5=3. Run1=1 was outside left but not directly involved. So the good pattern in original was runs2-5 = 2,3,2,3. It did not require deleting run1. The stack's neutral deletion of run2 was bad because run2 was left outer.

Thus a good deletion corresponds to a length-4 window in the original run sequence (after ignoring runs that are neutral-deleted outside the window?) Actually the outer runs can be original adjacent to the deleted pair if no deletions between. In example, yes original window runs2-5. In counterexample 1,2,1,3,2,3, the good deletion after neutral had outer runs? Original good deletion after neutral: sequence 1,2,3,2,3; good pattern 2,3,2,3 with outer runs original run2=2 and run6=3? Wait original runs: 1,2,1,3,2,3. After neutral deletion of run3=1, the good pattern was original runs2=2,4=3,5=2,6=3. So the outer runs were original run2 and run6, with run3 deleted between. Thus a good deletion can have outer runs that are not adjacent in original; intervening runs are neutral-deleted. So we need account for deletions of blocking runs.

This is reminiscent finding a maximum set of good deletions where each good deletion consumes two active runs and "protects" two outer runs; neutral deletions consume one active run and protect one outer run. We want maximize good count. This could be solved by DP over runs with states? Maybe because each run can be either deleted (as part of good/neutral) or survive. The operations are local and non-crossing, so interval DP? But N 2e5, need linear.

Let's look for another angle.

Recall original problem: answer = min operations. Maybe there is a known simple formula: answer = number of runs - maximum number of disjoint "alternating subsequences" of length 4? In example 1,2,3,2,3, max good patterns? There is one pattern 2,3,2,3. In counterexample 1,2,1,3,2,3, there is no length-4 alternating pattern in original? Wait original 1,2,1,3,2,3 has pattern? 1,2,1,3 no; 2,1,3,2 no; 1,3,2,3 no. But after deleting the middle 1, pattern 2,3,2,3 appears. This is like we can delete a run (1) that is between the left outer 2 and the pattern? Actually the run 1 at position3 was between original run2=2 and run4=3. It was deleted neutrally because it matched run1=1. So neutral deletion removed a run that was not part of the good pattern but blocked adjacency between left outer and the first middle? Wait good pattern after neutral: 2,3,2,3. The left outer is original run2=2, first middle is original run4=3, second middle original run5=2, right outer original run6=3. The deleted run3=1 was between left outer and first middle. So neutral deletion removed a run between the left outer and the deleted pair. This is like we can skip over runs that are "matched" with earlier runs.

This is starting to look like finding a maximum number of "abcabc" patterns with deletions of certain runs. Maybe can be solved by dynamic programming with a stack that only performs good deletions, but when a neutral opportunity appears, we need decide based on future. In example 1,2,3,2,3, the neutral opportunity at 1,2,3,2 should be skipped because the 2 is needed as left outer for future good. How to decide? Maybe we should not perform neutral deletions at all; instead, when a good pattern appears in the stack after pushing all runs, perform it. But in counterexample 1,2,1,3,2,3, no good pattern appears in original stack; we need neutral deletion to expose it. So we need a way to "skip" runs that are neutral-deletable.

Could model the process as reducing the run sequence by deleting runs that are "dominated" by a same-value run two positions away. This is similar to removing runs that have a matching value before them with one run between. The order of deletions might be determined by a stack that processes runs and maintains a reduced sequence where no neutral deletion is possible? But we saw neutral deletion can destroy good patterns, so maybe we should only perform neutral deletions that are "safe" (i.e., the run to delete is not part of any future good pattern). How to know?

Let's consider the final reduced sequence after all good deletions (and necessary neutral deletions). Neutral deletions remove runs that are not part of good pairs but block adjacency. They are essentially "garbage" runs that can be deleted at zero net cost if they have a same-value neighbor on one side after some deletions. This is reminiscent reducing a string by removing characters that are equal to a neighbor after removing a character between them. The maximum number of good deletions might be computable by a greedy algorithm that scans from left to right and maintains a stack, but only performs good deletions; neutral deletions are implicit by skipping? Let's see.

In counterexample 1,2,1,3,2,3, if we scan and don't perform neutral, stack becomes [1,2,1,3,2,3]. No top4 good. Maybe the correct rule is to only perform neutral deletions that delete the *rightmost* of the two equal runs (v2 in pattern a,b,a,c), not the left one. In 1,2,3,2, the neutral opportunity is v1==v3 (2==2), which would delete the left run (v1). That is unsafe. In 1,2,1,3, the neutral opportunity is v0==v2 (1==1), deleting the right run (v2), safe. So perhaps we should only perform neutral deletions where the run to delete is the later duplicate (right one), not the earlier duplicate. Because deleting the earlier duplicate removes a potential left outer for future patterns, while deleting the later duplicate removes a potential blocker/right duplicate. Is that always safe?

Second good swap: current runs values 1,2,3,2,3,2. Pattern 2,3,2,3 at runs2-5? Values runs2=2,3=3,4=2,5=3. Swap middle run3=3 and run4=2 (positions? current sequence 1,2,3,3,2,2,3,2. The good pattern 2,3,2,3 has middle runs 3 and2 with lengths2,2, so a swap would not reduce runs by2! Ah! The alternative is invalid because after first good, the outer runs became inactive/long, and the pattern for second good uses them as middle, but they are not singleton. The run-level reduction with active flags prevents this. Let's check: After first good deletion of runs4,5, the outer runs r3=3 and r6=2 become inactive (length2). Thus k remains1. Safe stack k1 was correct. Good.

So active flags save us. Greedy good might still be safe? Need test.

What about a case where skipping a good allows another good with active runs? Since good deletion marks outer runs inactive, it may block adjacent patterns. Greedy leftmost good might block a later good that uses one of its outer runs as middle, but those outer runs become inactive, so they couldn't be middle anyway. If a later good uses them as outer, inactive is fine. Thus performing a good deletion seems safe: it deletes two active runs and blocks two outer runs. Could the deleted runs have been used as outer for two future good deletions instead? A run can be outer for multiple future deletions while active; if deleted, lost. But a good deletion consumes two active runs; maybe those runs could have served as outer for two good deletions, yielding more. Need find counterexample.

In alternating length7, greedy good at runs2,3 blocked run4, but run4 served as outer for second good. Deleted runs2,3 could they have served as outer for other goods? Maybe not.

Let's search for a case where greedy good is suboptimal. We need two possible good deletions overlapping such that choosing left blocks a better combination. Since good deletion marks outer runs inactive but doesn't delete them; they can still be outer. It deletes the middle runs. If a future good deletion would use one of the deleted middle runs as an outer, it's impossible. Could a middle run be an outer for a future good? Yes, if it survives. But it's deleted. Maybe choosing a different good deletion preserves it and yields more.

Example sequence values: 1,2,1,2,3,2,3? Greedy good at 1,2,1,2 deletes runs2,3, blocks run1,4. Remaining 1,4,5,6,7? Values 1,2,3,2,3? run4=2 inactive, run5=3, run6=2, run7=3. Pattern 2,3,2,3 at runs4-7 but run4 inactive as left outer, okay! Middle run5=3, run6=2 active -> good! So k=2. Greedy fine.

What about 1,2,1,2,1,2? Greedy k1, max1.

What about 1,2,1,2,3,2,3,2,3? Greedy first good at 1,2,1,2, then maybe good at 2,3,2,3? Let's simulate:

I suspect good greedy is safe, but neutral greedy is not. We need an algorithm that performs good deletions greedily but handles neutral deletions with lookahead/only safe ones. In the counterexample 1,2,3,2,3, the good pattern was available after pushing the fifth run; if we had waited instead of neutral right, we'd get it. So we should not perform neutral right. What about neutral left? In 1,2,1,3,2,3, neutral left was needed and safe. Could neutral left ever be unsafe (destroy a future good)? Let's test.

Neutral left deletes the second of two equal runs with one run between (pattern a,b,a,c). The deleted run is the right duplicate. Could it be needed as an outer for a future good? It is the right duplicate; future good to the right might use it as left outer. If deleted, lost. Example original 1,2,1,2,3? Here top4 1,2,1,2 is good, not neutral. For neutral left, c!=b, so pattern 1,2,1,3. The second 1 (run3) could be left outer for a future good pattern 1,?,1,? if run4=3 and future? For it to be left outer, the pattern would be 1, A, 1, A. Since run4=3, A would need 3, and run5=1, run6=3. Original 1,2,1,3,1,3. Let's test if neutral left deleting run3 is bad.

Sequence: 1,2,1,3,1,3 all active.
Options:
- Greedy neutral left on 1,2,1,3 deletes run3 -> stack [1i,2,3,1,3]. Top4 [2,3,1,3] no. k0.
- Skip neutral, maybe good pattern using run3 as left outer: runs3=1, run4=3, run5=1, run6=3 -> pattern 1,3,1,3 is good! It would delete runs4,5, k1. So neutral left would destroy a good pattern! So no neutral greedy.

We need a fundamentally different approach.

Let's step back. The run reduction system with good/neutral is complex. Maybe there is a direct DP/combinatorial formula.

Recall answer = min inv(P)+runs(P). We need maximize adj_eq(P)-inv(P). This is a global optimization. Maybe it can be solved by dynamic programming on the run sequence with states representing "open" good patterns? The examples suggest good patterns can be nested/overlapping with neutral deletions.

Let's analyze the structure of good deletions in terms of original runs and active flags.

A good deletion consumes two singleton runs that are adjacent in the current sequence. The outer runs become inactive. This is exactly like matching pairs of runs in a non-crossing way, where each matched pair (the two deleted runs) is enclosed by two outer runs that are not deleted. Additionally, neutral deletions remove singleton runs that are not matched, but they must be "covered" by an outer run of same value? In the examples, neutral-deleted runs were between an outer run and a matched pair, and had same value as an outer run.

Maybe we can formulate as: We want to select a set of disjoint pairs of runs to delete (good pairs). For each selected pair (i,j) with i<j, they must be adjacent after deleting all runs between them that are not selected? Actually if there are unselected runs between them, they wouldn't be adjacent. Thus all runs between i and j must also be deleted (either as part of other good pairs or neutral). This implies selected good pairs form a non-crossing matching, and the intervals between paired runs are fully deleted. This is like a context-free grammar.

Let's define a reduction of a substring to a single "boundary" run? A good deletion of pattern Y, X, Y, X reduces to Y, X (the outer runs), with the middle X,Y deleted. This is similar to the string rewrite Y X Y X -> Y X. Neutral deletion Y X Y Z -> Y X Z (delete second Y) or Z X Y X -> Z Y X (delete first X). These rules are exactly the rules of reducing a word by removing "alternating" substrings. The number of good reductions might be equal to the maximum number of disjoint occurrences of a pattern in a reduction to a "2-run" normal form.

Maybe we can solve with dynamic programming over values? Since values up to N, but runs total N. The rewrite rules only depend on equality of values. This is reminiscent the problem of finding the maximum number of "abab" subsequences after deleting some characters, which can be solved by a stack that maintains a reduced sequence where no pattern a,b,a exists? Let's think.

If we ignore active flags and neutral costs, the value sequence reduction rules:
- Y X Y X -> Y X (good)
- Y X Y Z -> Y X Z (neutral left)
- Z X Y X -> Z Y X (neutral right)
These are equivalent to the single rule: Y X Y -> Y X? Wait neutral left deletes the second Y in Y X Y Z, leaving Y X Z. This is exactly deleting the last character of a pattern Y X Y (the second Y) when followed by Z != X. Neutral right deletes the first X in Z X Y X, leaving Z Y X, which is deleting the first character of pattern X Y X when preceded by Z != Y. Both are forms of deleting a character that is equal to a character two positions away, with the intervening character singleton. In value-only terms, the rule is: in a substring a b a, delete either the first or last a? Actually neutral left deletes the last a; neutral right deletes the first a. Good deletes the middle b and last a? Wait good a b a b deletes middle b,a, leaving a,b. That is like deleting the inner two of a b a b.

Maybe the maximum number of good deletions equals the maximum number of times we can apply the value-only rule a b a b -> a b, where neutral deletions are free and can be used to make patterns adjacent. This is equivalent to reducing the run value sequence by repeatedly deleting characters that are equal to a neighbor two positions away? This might be related to the "signature" of the sequence under the equivalence relation generated by deleting "palindromic" substrings of length 3? Let's explore.

Consider the value sequence only (ignore lengths). The neutral deletion of the last a in a b a c (c!=b) removes a character that is a "duplicate" of the character two positions left. This is similar to canceling a b a to a b (if followed by c). If we apply all possible neutral deletions greedily from left to right, we might get a unique normal form. In 1,2,1,3,1,3, neutral left on 1,2,1,3 would delete run3=1, leading to 1,2,3,1,3, which destroyed good 1,3,1,3. But if we instead delete run4=3? Pattern 2,1,3,1? That's neutral right deleting run2? Hmm.

Maybe the correct normal form should not delete a character if it is part of a future a b a b. This is like finding longest sequence of good reductions, which might be equivalent to finding a maximum matching in a graph of equal values separated by one run.

Let's look at the problem from the pick model again. Maybe there is a known DP:
We have runs. A good swap corresponds to swapping two adjacent singleton runs of different values that are surrounded by their values. This is exactly a "move" in a sorting network. The total number of operations might be N - length of longest "alternating subsequence"? Let's compute examples:
- 1,2,3,2,3: N=5, answer4, saving1. Longest something?
- 1,2,1,3,1,3: N=6, answer5, saving1.
- 1,2,1,3,2,3: N=6, answer5, saving1.
- Alternating length11, saving3 = floor((m-1)/3)? For runs all singleton, answer = N - floor((m-1)/3). This is like maximum number of disjoint "abcabc"? Not.

Maybe the answer can be expressed as N - maximum number of disjoint adjacent pairs in the original sequence after RLE? Let's compute for alternating length m, max good reductions floor((m-1)/3). This is also the maximum number of disjoint length-4 alternating patterns with start positions 1,4,7? For m=11, starts1,4,7? Patterns positions1-4,4-7,7-10? That's 3. For m=8, starts1,4? 2. For m=6, starts1? 1. So max = floor((m-1)/3). This is like selecting every third run.

For general sequences, maybe the maximum number of good reductions equals the maximum number of disjoint "alternating quadruples" in the run value sequence, where quadruple values a,b,a,b and the middle two runs have length1, and quadruples can overlap only in a certain way? In alternating, selected quadruples can share boundaries? Starts1,4,7 for m=11: pattern1 positions1-4, pattern2 positions4-7 share run4? But run4 is right outer for first and left outer for second, allowed if inactive. So they overlap at one run. This is like selecting starts every 3.

In 1,2,3,2,3, the good quadruple is runs2-5 = 2,3,2,3. In 1,2,1,3,2,3, after deleting run3, good quadruple is runs2,4,5,6 = 2,3,2,3. It is outside the quadruple? Actually it lies between left outer run2 and first middle run4. It is deleted because it's a duplicate of run1, not part of the good pattern. So a good reduction can skip over some runs that are neutral-deleted.

This suggests a DP over runs where each run can be in one of three states: deleted as neutral, deleted as part of good pair, or surviving. Neutral deletions are free (not counted) but cost swaps cancel. We want maximize good pairs. The constraints for a good pair (i,j) that are adjacent after deleting all runs between them:
- Let L be nearest surviving run to the left, R nearest surviving to the right.
- Values: val(L) = val(j), val(R) = val(i).
- Runs i and j must be singleton (active) at time of deletion; if they were outer in previous deletions, they'd be inactive, so they must not have served as outer before. Thus a run can be either deleted (good/neutral) or survive as outer; if it survives, it may be outer for multiple good deletions but becomes inactive after first, which doesn't prevent outer role. So surviving runs can be outer any number of times.
- Neutral-deleted runs are singleton at deletion and have a same-value surviving/outer run on one side with one singleton between? This is messy.

Maybe we can solve the original optimization with dynamic programming over values using the fact that N 2e5 but values up to N. Could use min-cost max-flow? No.

Let's revisit the NP-hard concern. If the problem allowed arbitrary P, it might be NP-hard, but the operation 2 prefix deletion maybe imposes that P is not arbitrary? Wait we proved all swaps before deletions, so P arbitrary.
