Let's consider the original operation cost from another perspective. Each operation 2 deletes a block. The total number of operations = number of blocks + swaps. If we think of each element except the first in each block as "saved" by being adjacent to previous same element in P. To make two same elements adjacent in P, we may need to swap them across intervening elements. The net saving of making a pair of equal elements adjacent is 1 (one less deletion) minus the number of inversions created. In the run reduction, a good swap creates two equal adjacencies at cost1, net saving1. Neutral swap creates one equal adjacency at cost1, net0. Bad swaps create none/negative. So net saving is exactly (number of equal adjacencies in P) - (inversions). Each equal adjacency in P corresponds to a pair of consecutive occurrences of same value in P. Since occurrences of same value appear in original order, equal adjacencies connect consecutive occurrences of a value in P (not necessarily consecutive in original; other values may have been moved out). Inversions are pairs of elements whose order changed.

Maybe we can model this as: We choose for each value some of its consecutive occurrences to be "linked" in P. If we link two occurrences of value v that have k other elements between them in original, to make them adjacent in P, all those k elements must be moved to either before the first or after the second. The minimal inversions? For two occurrences p<q of same value, if we want them adjacent in P with no other elements between, the elements between p and q in original must be placed either before p or after q in P. The number of inversions created is at least min(#elements between placed before p? Actually if an element between p and q is placed before p, it crosses p; if placed after q, it crosses q. Either way one inversion per between element? Let's compute: Original order p < between < q. Target order either between before p < p < q (inversion with p) or p < q < between (inversion with q). So each between element creates at least1 inversion. Thus linking two occurrences costs at least the number of elements between them in original. In sample3, linking pos1 and pos3 (1s) cost1 (pos2 between), linking pos5 and pos7 cost1, linking pos9 and pos11? Actually final P had equal adjacencies: (pos1,pos3) cost1, (pos5,pos7) cost1, (pos9,pos11)? Wait final P after 3 swaps had equal adjacencies: 1s at pos1,pos3; 2s at pos2,pos4? Let's map final P values: 1,1,2,2,1,1,2,2,2,1,1. Equal adjacencies: 1-pos1&3, 2-pos2&4, 1-pos5&7, 2-pos6&8&10? Actually 2s at pos6,pos8,pos10 consecutive (two adjacencies), 1s at pos9,pos11. Inversions total3. Equal adjacencies6. Net saving3. The cost to link pos1&3 is1, pos5&7 is1, pos9&11 is1. The 2s links maybe cost0 because they became adjacent after moving 1s? This is like selecting links between consecutive occurrences of same value; each link costs the number of other elements between them that are not already moved by other links. This is similar to finding a maximum weight matching in a graph of occurrences, maybe solvable by interval scheduling.

Specifically, consider each value's occurrences in original order. A link between occurrence j and j+1 of same value, if they become adjacent in P, saves 1 deletion. The cost in inversions is the number of elements of other values between them that are not placed outside the pair. But if those elements are themselves linked/moved, costs can be shared. This is like choosing a set of intervals (between consecutive same-value occurrences) to "contract"; intervals can be nested/disjoint, and cost is the number of elements inside not covered by other contractions? This is exactly the run reduction.

Maybe there is a known greedy: The maximum net saving equals the maximum number of disjoint intervals [l,r] such that A_l = A_r and ...? Let's test. A good swap pattern Y,X,Y,X corresponds to linking the two Ys? Actually after swap, equal adjacencies are Y-Y (left outer and first middle? Wait pattern Y,X,Y,X -> after swap Y,Y,X,X. Equal adjacencies: left Y with first middle Y (they were originally separated by X? Original Y, X, Y, X; after swap Y,Y,X,X. The link is between original run1 Y and run3 Y, with run2 X between. Cost1 (X moved after). Also link between original run2 X and run4 X, with run3 Y between? Cost1? But one swap created both links at cost1, net saving1. So a good swap corresponds to two links sharing the same swap cost. The interval for Y link is (run1,run3) containing run2; interval for X link is (run2,run4) containing run3. They overlap. Cost of swap is 1, not sum of interval lengths (which would be2). So links can share cost.

This is getting too deep.

Let's use the pick model and try to design a DP over positions with state = previous output value. Since cost of picking an element = number of remaining before it. This is similar to "minimum cost topological sort" with chains per value. There is a known greedy: At each step, pick the leftmost remaining element unless picking another element of the same value as previous can save more. The optimal might be found by a priority queue? Let's explore.

In the pick model, we process output order. At each step, we have a set of remaining elements in original order. If we pick the first remaining element, cost0. If we pick a later element, cost = its rank. Reward if same as previous. This is equivalent to: We can either delete the current first element (cost0, no reward unless same as previous) or "jump" to some later element paying its rank, then maybe delete some elements before it later. Since cost is rank, jumping over elements is expensive. The optimal might be to always pick either the first element or an element of the same value as previous that is as early as possible. This suggests a stack/queue algorithm.

Let's test sample3 with pick model: output P = 1,1,2,2,1,1,2,2,2,1,1. At each step, we picked either first remaining or an early same-value element. The total rank cost3. This is like we scan original and occasionally "swap" adjacent pairs to group.

Maybe we can formulate as: We have a sequence; each operation in pick model:
- Delete first element (cost0).
- Delete any element with cost equal to its current index (number before it).
Reward if same as previous deleted.
We want min cost - reward. This is reminiscent "minimum cost to delete all elements with rewards for consecutive same values" and can be solved by dynamic programming with a segment tree? At each step, the state is the set of remaining elements, huge. But maybe optimal policy is to only delete the first element or an element immediately after the first? Because deleting an element with rank r>1 costs r; you could instead delete the r elements before it first (cost maybe 0 for first, etc.) and then delete it cost0, but you might lose reward. In sample3, to output pos3 after pos1, we paid rank1 (pos2 before it) instead of deleting pos2 first. This was to get reward for same value. So we may skip over a block of elements to get reward, paying the number skipped. This is like we can choose to "save" a deletion by paying the number of elements skipped.

This is exactly the problem of finding a maximum weight path in a DAG where nodes are occurrences, edges from an occurrence to a later occurrence of same value have weight 1 - (#elements between), and we can also delete elements individually. Maybe the net saving equals maximum weight of a set of non-crossing edges between same-value occurrences, where edge weight = 1 - distance? Let's test.

If we link two occurrences of same value in P (make them adjacent), the saving is 1 (one less deletion) minus the number of elements between them that are not already skipped? In sample3, links between pos1&3 (distance1, weight0? 1-1=0), pos5&7 (0), pos9&11 (0), plus links between 2s maybe pos2&4? Wait final equal adjacencies: 1-1 at pos1&3, 2-2 at pos2&4, 1-1 at pos5&7, 2-2 at pos6&8, 2-2 at pos8&10, 1-1 at pos9&11. Weights for each link: 1 - (#elements between) = 1 -1 =0 for all? Then total saving0, but actual saving3. Because links share skipped elements: pos2 is between pos1&3 and also linked with pos4? The cost of swapping pos2 and pos3 created both links for cost1. So edges share cost.

This is like selecting a set of adjacent swaps; each swap can create up to two links. The maximum net saving is the maximum number of swaps that create two links, which is the run reduction.

Process runs left to right with stack.
After pushing a run, while stack length >= 3:
Let top3 = stack[-3], stack[-2], stack[-1] values a,b,c.
If a == c and middle active (stack[-2] active):
    # We have a palindrome a,b,a. Need lookahead? But we already pushed c=a. The next run is not known yet. If we delete now (neutral left), we might destroy a future good a,b,a,b. In 1,2,1,3,1,3, deleting now destroyed good. So we need lookahead to the next run before deciding. Since we process online, we can delay deletion until we see the next run. But if we don't delete now, the stack contains a,b,a. When the next run d arrives:
       if d == b: good deletion (delete b,a, count)
       else: neutral deletion (delete top a)
This suggests we should not reduce immediately on palindrome; we should wait for the next run. But the next run may be far? The top3 palindrome is at the end; the next run will be pushed immediately in the loop. So we can handle when pushing each run: before pushing, check if current stack top3 is palindrome a,b,a and the new run d is known. If d == b, perform good; else if d != b, perform neutral (delete top a) and then maybe recheck. This is equivalent to processing top4 after pushing d:
- If top4 values a,b,a,b: good.
- Else if top3 a,b,a and new d != b: neutral left delete top a.
- Else if top4 values a,b,c,b (neutral right): do nothing.
This is exactly the safe algorithm (only neutral left, no neutral right). Let's test on 1,2,1,3,1,3:

Given the complexity, maybe the intended solution is indeed a DP with O(N) using last occurrence positions. Let's try to formulate DP for maximum saving.

From the pick model, net saving = equal_adj(P) - inv(P). Equal adjacencies in P are between consecutive occurrences of same value. Suppose we decide to link two occurrences of value v at original positions p<q. If they are consecutive in P, then all elements between p and q in original must be placed either before p or after q in P. The inversion cost contributed by these between elements is at least 1 each, but if they are also linked/placed, costs shared. This is like selecting a set of links forming a non-crossing matching on the line, where each link between same-value occurrences has profit 1 and cost equal to the number of unmatched elements inside? Actually if we select a set of links (same-value pairs) that are non-crossing, and every element inside a link must be either inside a nested link or moved out. The total inversion cost might equal the number of elements that are not covered by any link? Let's test sample3.

Function reduce_good(stack, k):
    while len(stack)>=4:
        v0,a0 = stack[-4]; v1,a1=stack[-3]; v2,a2=stack[-2]; v3,a3=stack[-1]
        if a1 and a2 and v0==v2 and v1==v3:
            stack[-4][1]=False; stack[-1][1]=False
            d=stack.pop(); stack.pop(); stack.pop(); stack.append(d)
            k+=1
        else:
            break
    return k

Function reduce_neutral(stack):
    # assumes no good at top
    if len(stack)>=4:
        v0,a0=stack[-4]; v1,a1=stack[-3]; v2,a2=stack[-2]; v3,a3=stack[-1]
        if a1 and a2:
            if v0==v2 and v1!=v3:
                # neutral left delete C
                stack[-4][1]=False
                d=stack.pop()  # D
                stack.pop()    # C
                stack.append(d)
                return True
            elif v1==v3 and v0!=v2:
                # neutral right delete B
                stack[-1][1]=False
                d=stack.pop()  # D
                c=stack.pop()  # C
                stack.pop()    # B
                stack.append(c)
                stack.append(d)
                return True
    return False

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []

    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it)
        A = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]

        # Run-length encoding
        runs = []
        for x in A:
            if runs and runs[-1][0] == x:
                runs[-1][1] += 1
            else:
                runs.append([x, 1])

        m = len(runs)
        stack = []  # elements are [value, is_singleton]
        good = 0

        def try_good():
            nonlocal good
            changed = False
            while len(stack) >= 4:
                v0, a0 = stack[-4]
                v1, a1 = stack[-3]
                v2, a2 = stack[-2]
                v3, a3 = stack[-1]
                if a1 and a2 and v0 == v2 and v1 == v3:
                    stack[-4][1] = False
                    stack[-1][1] = False
                    d = stack.pop()
                    stack.pop()
                    stack.pop()
                    stack.append(d)
                    good += 1
                    changed = True
                else:
                    break
            return changed

        def try_neutral():
            if len(stack) < 4:
                return False
            v0, a0 = stack[-4]
            v1, a1 = stack[-3]
            v2, a2 = stack[-2]
            v3, a3 = stack[-1]
            if not (a1 and a2):
                return False

            # good case is handled before, so here exactly one side matches
            if v0 == v2 and v1 != v3:
                # delete the second middle run
                stack[-4][1] = False
                d = stack.pop()
                stack.pop()
                stack.append(d)
                return True

            if v1 == v3 and v0 != v2:
                # delete the first middle run
                stack[-1][1] = False
                d = stack.pop()
                c = stack.pop()
                stack.pop()
                stack.append(c)
                stack.append(d)
                return True

            return False

        for val, length in runs:
            stack.append([val, length == 1])
            try_good()

        # After all runs are read, we may still need neutral reductions
        # to expose further good reductions.
        while True:
            if try_good():
                continue
            if not try_neutral():
                break

        ans = m - good
        out.append(str(ans))

    print("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
